=== chlordiazePOXIDE/Amitriptyline (Merck Manual Professional) ===

NURSING QUICK-REFERENCE SUMMARY
Auto-generated on 2026-09-25 from the prescribing information in this file; full details follow below.

Drug Class: Chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline are used together in an oral preparation to treat moderate to severe depression associated with moderate to severe anxiety.

Routes: oral; Common adult doses: 60 mg/day PO chlordiazepoxide and 150 mg/day PO amitriptyline.

Common Side Effects (excluding routine GI upset): abnormal dreams, confusion, dizziness, drowsiness, euphoria, fatigue, hallucinations, impaired cognition, lethargy, mania, restlessness, weakness, ataxia, eeg changes, paresthesias, pseudoparkinsonism, seizures, syncope, tremor, arrhythmia exacerbation, av block, cardiomyopathy, hypertension, hypotension, myocardial infarction, orthostatic hypotension, palpitations, qt prolongation, sinus tachycardia, stroke

In an Overdose: Although all tricyclic antidepressants are thought to be proarrhythmic after acute overdoses, at therapeutic doses, their actions on the conducting system of the heart may vary. Seizures are reported primarily in susceptible individuals, with overdosage of tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs), or with abrupt cessation of benzodiazepines.

Key Nursing Considerations: Contraindications/precautions: NOTE: This monograph discusses the use of the amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide combination product for the management of major depressive disorder associated with anxiety.

Brand Names
  - Limbitrol
  - Limbitrol DS

  Indications
    Routes
      Heading
      Oral dosage
      Route Age Groups
        Heading
        Adults
        Dosage Description
        Initially, 1 regular strength tablet (containing chlordiazepoxide 5 mg with amitriptyline 12.5 mg) or 1 double strength (DS) tablet (containing chlordiazepoxide 10 mg with amitriptyline 25 mg) PO 3 to 4 times per day. Geriatric and debilitated adults should start at lower dose. Adjust dose as tolerated and required; some patients may respond to a single dose at bedtime. The larger portion of the total daily dose may be taken at bedtime. Max: 60 mg chlordiazepoxide with 150 mg of amitriptyline per day (e.g., up to 6 DS tablets/day).
    Heading
    For the treatment of moderate to severe depression associated with moderate to severe anxiety

Contraindications And Precautions
  Headline
  Contraindications/Warnings/Precautions
  General Information
  NOTE: This monograph discusses the use of the amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide combination product for the management of major depressive disorder associated with anxiety. Clinicians may wish to consult the individual drug monographs for more information.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide is contraindicated in patients with either a tricyclic antidepressant hypersensitivity or a benzodiazepine hypersensitivity. Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) are known to produce an allergic response in some patients. There appears to be cross-sensitivity with TCAs, and caution should be used when changing from one tricyclic antidepressant to another. Alternative therapy should be considered in those patients. Patients allergic to tricyclic antidepressants may also display cross-sensitivity to carbamazepine (i.e., use caution in patients with carbamazepine hypersensitivity), maprotiline, or amoxapine.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide is contraindicated in patients receiving a monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI therapy). Hyperpyretic crises, severe convulsions, and deaths have occurred in patients receiving a tricyclic antidepressant and a monoamine oxidase inhibitor simultaneously (see Drug Interactions). An interval of 14 days is recommended between cessation of an irreversible MAOI agent and initiation of tricyclic antidepressant therapy and vice-versa.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide can cause physical and psychological dependence, and should be used with extreme caution in patients with known, suspected, or a history of substance abuse. Following prolonged therapy with high doses, abrupt discontinuation of amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be avoided because it could precipitate symptoms of cholinergic rebound due to the TCA component (such as nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea) or benzodiazepine withdrawal symptoms, especially following high dose or prolonged therapy (see Adverse Effects). However, benzodiazepine dependence can occur following administration of therapeutic doses for as few as 1—2 weeks and withdrawal symptoms may be seen following the discontinuation of therapy. Patients with a history of a seizure disorder should not be withdrawn abruptly from benzodiazepines due to the risk of precipitating seizures; status epilepticus has also been reported. Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be withdrawn slowly, using a gradual dosage-tapering schedule. During withdrawal, the greatest risk of seizure appears to be during the first 24 to 72 hours. Clinicians should be aware that the use of flumazenil may increase the risk of seizures, particularly in long-term users of benzodiazepines and in cyclic antidepressant overdoses (see Flumazenil monograph). If seizures occur during therapy with amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide, the drug should be discontinued.
 
Occasionally, pre-existing depression may emerge or worsen with the use of benzodiazepines. In addition, all effective antidepressants can transform depression into mania or hypomania in predisposed individuals. It is generally believed (though not established in controlled trials) that treating such an episode with an antidepressant alone may increase the likelihood of precipitation of a mixed/manic episode in patients at risk for bipolar disorder. If a patient develops symptoms suggestive of mania, amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be held and the appropriate therapy to treat the manic symptoms initiated. Additionally, depression may be the presenting symptom of a mixed/manic episode of bipolar disorder. Patients should be adequately screened for bipolar disorder prior to initiating an antidepressant. Such screening should include a detailed psychiatric history, including a family history of suicide, bipolar disorder, and depression. Patients with depression or comorbid depression in the setting of other psychiatric illness being treated with antidepressants should be observed for clinical worsening and suicidality, especially during the initial few months of a course of drug therapy, or at times of dose changes. Caregivers should be advised to closely observe the patient on a daily basis and to communicate immediately with the prescriber the emergence of agitation, irritability, unusual changes in behavior, or suicidality. It should be noted that amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide is not approved for use in treating bipolar depression. Use TCAs with caution in patients with psychotic disorders (e.g., schizophrenia), as the antidepressant may activate psychotic symptoms in some individuals.
 
There is limited information on the safety and efficacy of amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide in children, and in general its use is not recommended due to lack of ability to effectively titrate doses and multiple safety concerns. As monotherapy, the safe and effective use of chlordiazepoxide in children under 6 years old has not been established and is not recommended in this age group. In October 2004, the FDA directed manufacturers of all antidepressants to include a boxed warning detailing the risk of suicide in pediatric patients. A causal role has been established for antidepressants in inducing suicidality in pediatric patients. The risk of suicidality for these drugs was identified in a pooled analysis of 24 placebo-controlled trials (n=4400) lasting up to 16 weeks in pediatric patients with major depressive disorder (MDD), obsessive compulsive disorder (OCD), or other psychiatric disorders. The analysis showed a greater risk of suicidality during the first few months of treatment in those receiving antidepressants (SSRIs and others). The average risk of such events on drug was 4% and 2% for placebo; however, no suicides occurred in these trials. Pooled analysis of short-term clinical trials during early phase treatment with SSRIs and other antidepressants in young adults (18 to 24 years) also showed an increased risk of suicidal thinking and behavior. The clinical need for an antidepressant in children or young adults for any use must be weighed against the risk of increased suicidality; patients who are started on therapy should be observed closely for clinical worsening, suicidality, or unusual changes in behavior, particularly within the first few months of starting therapy or at the time of dose increase or decrease; such observation would generally include at least weekly face-to-face contact with patients during the first 4 weeks of treatment, then every other week visits for the next 4 weeks, then at 12 weeks, and as clinically indicated beyond 12 weeks; additional contact by telephone may be appropriate between visits. It is unknown if the suicidality risk in children and young adults extends to longer-term therapy (i.e., beyond several months). The possibility of a suicide attempt is inherent in patients with depressive symptoms, whether these occur in primary depression or in association with another primary psychiatric disorder such as OCD. All patients with a history of suicidal ideation or behaviors and those with a prominence of suicidal ideation prior to treatment are considered at an increased risk for suicidal ideation or attempts, and should be closely monitored during treatment with amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide. In patients who exhibit changes in symptoms, worsening of depression or suicidality, a decision should be made to change or discontinue treatment. If discontinuing, medication should be tapered as rapidly as possible, but with recognition that rapid discontinuation can also cause adverse symptoms. All antidepressants should be prescribed in the smallest quantity consistent with good patient management in order to reduce the risk of overdose. Children are generally more sensitive to the CNS effects of the benzodiazepines. Paradoxical reactions, including acute rage, stimulation and excitement have been reported in hyperactive aggressive pediatric patients and should be monitored for if chlordiazepoxide therapy is instituted. It is recommended that amitriptyline be used with caution in children with a known family history of heart disease, or who are taking other medications that might cause drug interactions (see Drug Interactions). QTc interval prolongation, tachycardias, and other side effects have been reported in children who have taken tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs); there are rare reports of deaths due to cardiovascular side effects. Routine cardiovascular monitoring has been suggested for children receiving TCAs due to the potential of these agents to produce adverse cardiac effects.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide can induce significant sedation, particularly during the initiation of treatment. Patients should use caution when driving or operating machinery until they are aware of the effects of the medication. Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be avoided in patients with ethanol intoxication. Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be used with caution in patients with a history of alcoholism or who may use alcohol or other sedative medications because the depressant effects on the CNS can be potentiated (see Drug Interactions). Decreased mental alertness can occur. Anterograde amnesia has been reported with the use of benzodiazepines. Use of amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be avoided in patients with coma, head trauma, or shock also due to worsening CNS depression.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide dose selection should generally be cautious in the elderly patient, starting at the low end of the dosage range (see Dosage). Geriatric patients are particularly sensitive to the peripheral and central anticholinergic side effects of tricyclic antidepressants. The impairment of cognitive and motor function may be more marked in this patient group. Some authors have recommended against the use of amitriptyline in the elderly because of the strong sedative and anticholinergic properties of the drug. Additionally, benzodiazepines have been associated with an increased incidence of falls in the elderly, and a similar risk may exist with the TCAs. The concomitant use of other psychotropic agents should be approached with caution in the elderly. Analysis of short-term studies in geriatric patients aged 65 years and older showed a decline in the risk of suicidal thinking and behavior during early phase treatment with SSRIs and other antidepressants, contrary to the data obtained in children and young adults.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide is contraindicated in patients who are in the acute recovery phase following acute myocardial infarction; this could cause sudden death. Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be used with caution in patients with any cardiac disease (e.g., heart failure, history of myocardial infarction), because of the alterations in ECG patterns or heart rhythm that may occur with the administration of tricyclic antidepressant drugs (TCAs). Many adverse cardiovascular effects are associated with the use of TCAs, and they can lead to complete cardiac collapse and sudden death. Although the risk of cardiovascular adverse events is higher after acute overdose, patients with cardiovascular disease should be closely monitored via ECGs and clinical exams. Amitriptyline may cause orthostatic hypotension, particularly in the initial dose titration. Tricyclic antidepressants should be avoided in patients with QT prolongation or familial histories of long-QT syndromes or in those patients with cardiac conduction defects (e.g., cardiac arrhythmias, AV block, bundle-branch block). Myocardial infarction and stroke have been reported with the use of TCAs.
 
Anticholinergic effects appear most frequently and cause the greatest morbidity in geriatric patients. The anticholinergic effects of tricyclic antidepressants limit their use in patients with decreased GI motility or GI obstruction, and this effect can occur with use of amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide. The anticholinergic effects of amitriptyline may be significant and are additive with other anticholinergic medications. Tricyclic antidepressants can induce or exacerbate hiatal hernia, and can cause paralytic ileus or constipation. Patients who have increased intraocular pressure or closed-angle glaucoma, benign prostatic hypertrophy, GI disease, gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD), or urinary retention should be treated with caution because of the anticholinergic activity of tricyclic antidepressants. Additionally, benzodiazepines are often listed as contraindicated in patients with acute closed-angle glaucoma. The mechanistic rational for this contraindication has been questioned, as benzodiazepines do not have antimuscarinic activity and do not raise intraocular pressure. Benzodiazepines may be used in patients with open-angle glaucoma who are receiving appropriate therapy. The package labeling of amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide does not specifically list closed-angle glaucoma as a contraindication.
 
The anticholinergic effects of amitriptyline may increase lens discomfort for wearers of contact lenses. Mydriasis, disturbance of accommodation, and dry eyes may contribute to blurred vision and lens intolerance. The use of lubricating drops may be necessary.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be used with caution in patients with Parkinson's disease or neuromuscular disease (e.g., muscular dystrophy, myotonia, or myasthenia gravis) as these conditions can be exacerbated. Patients with late stage Parkinson's disease may experience worsening of their psychosis or impaired cognition with administration of benzodiazepines. TCAs and/or benzodiazepines may also cause incoordination or paradoxical reactions that may worsen symptoms of Parkinson's disease. Use cautiously in patients with organic brain syndromes (e.g., dementia) or other types of psychosis.
 
Patients with respiratory depression, sleep apnea, or pulmonary disease such as severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) should be treated cautiously with amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide because of the CNS-depressant effects of this drug. Additionally, asthma can be aggravated by administering tricyclic antidepressants to patients with the disease.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide therapy should be discontinued several days before elective surgery because of the risk of hypertensive episodes with TCAs.
 
On rare occasions, agranulocytosis, thrombocytopenia, eosinophilia, leukopenia, or purpura have been reported with tricyclic antidepressants. Any patient with symptoms of blood dyscrasia (sore throat, fever, bruising, etc.) should have immediate laboratory studies performed and suitable therapy initiated. Use tricyclic antidepressants cautiously in patients with preexisting hematological disease.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be used with extreme caution in patients with hepatic impairment or hepatic disease. Tricyclic antidepressants have caused reversible hepatitis and jaundice, as well as hepatic failure and death when tricyclic antidepressants were continued. Liver-function tests should be performed and the drug discontinued if there is persistent elevation of enzymes. Metabolism of amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide may be altered in patients with hepatic impairment; the elimination half-life of chlordiazepoxide can be prolonged, possibly resulting in toxicity. Additionally, chlordiazepoxide metabolites may accumulate leading to severe and prolonged stupor in patients with biliary cirrhosis or hepatitis. Patients with hepatic disease should receive reduced initial dosages and be closely monitored for tolerability.
 
Patients may be more prone to sunburn during therapy with a tricyclic antidepressant, as contained in amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide. Suitable precautions should be taken prior to sunlight (UV) exposure, such as wearing long-sleeved clothing and a hat, and using sunscreens.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be used with caution in patients who have hyperthyroidism or are receiving thyroid drugs. Concomitant use of tricyclic antidepressants with thyroid drugs can produce cardiac dysrhythmias. Hypothyroidism that is untreated will prevent adequate response to antidepressant therapy. Thyroid agents also can accelerate the onset of the response to tricyclic antidepressants.
 
Tricyclic antidepressants, such as amitriptyline, can affect blood glucose concentrations because of their effect on the endocrine system. Use Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide with caution in patients with diabetes mellitus.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide can lower the seizure threshold. Because of a potential increased risk of seizures, tricyclic antidepressants should not be used during intrathecal radiographic contrast administration. Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide therapy should be discontinued 48 hours before and not restarted for at least 24 hours after myelography.
 
Both amitriptyline and chlordiazepoxide are classified as FDA pregnancy risk category D. Chlordiazepoxide should not be administered during the first trimester of pregnancy as positive evidence of human fetal risk exists. There is rarely sufficient urgency to justify the use of minor tranquilizers during pregnancy. Additionally, neonatal flaccidity has been reported in an infant whose mother was using benzodiazepines during pregnancy. Patients should also be told about the risks to the neonate due to TCA use: possible fetal abnormality, delayed development, or withdrawal symptoms at birth. Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide is not recommended for use during pregnancy, unless the possible benefits outweigh the risks. Alternate antidepressants may prove to be more safe.
 
Amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide should be avoided in breast-feeding in most instances. Chlordiazepoxide is distributed into breast milk and can cause sedation, feeding difficulties, and weight loss in the nursing infant. The use of chlordiazepoxide monotherapy during breast-feeding is generally not recommended. Tricyclic antidepressants are also excreted into breast milk. Amitriptyline is listed by the American Academy of Pediatrics as a drug whose effect on the nursing infant is not known but may be of concern, particularly with prolonged exposure.
 
Tobacco smoking has been shown to increase the clearance of TCAs by inducing hepatic microsomal enzymes. The effect of tobacco on hepatic microsomal enzymes is not related to the nicotine component, so sudden smoking cessation may result in a reduced clearance of TCAs and increased TCA effects, despite the initiation of nicotine replacement products.
 
The administration of chlordiazepoxide can exacerbate acute intermittent porphyria, so the drug should be used with caution in patients with this condition.
 
Specific dosing guidelines for amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide in patients with renal impairment are not available. In patients with renal failure (CrCl < 10 ml/min), it is suggested to reduce the dose of single agent chlordiazepoxide by at least 50%. As amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide is only available as a fixed-dose combination product, it may not be possible to adjust the dose of the chlordiazepoxide component for patients with renal failure. In patients with renal failure, it may be most prudent to avoid the use of amitriptyline; chlordiazepoxide. Patients with renal impairment should be carefully monitored during prolonged treatment with benzodiazepines to avoid the adverse reactions that occur from accumulation.
  Contraindications And Precautions
    Headline
    Drug Interactions
    General Information
    The coadministration of certain medications may lead to harm and require avoidance or therapy modification; review all drug interactions prior to concomitant use of other medications.
    Headline
    Hypersensitivity
    General Information
    This medication is contraindicated in patients with a history of hypersensitivity to it or any of its components. This drug combination is contraindicated in patients with hypersensitivity to either benzodiazepines or tricyclic antidepressants. People with a hypersensitivity to amitriptyline may experience a cross-sensitivity reaction to other dibenzazepines, other tricyclic antidepressants, or to carbamazepine. The potential for cross-reactivity to similar compounds such as cyclobenzaprine or tetracyclic antidepressants (e.g., maprotiline, amoxapine) has not been established.
    Headline
    adolescents, children, suicidal ideation
    General Information
    The safe and effective use of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline has not been established in pediatric patients less than 18 years of age. In a pooled analysis of placebo-controlled trials of antidepressants (n = 4,500 pediatrics and 77,000 adults), there was an increased risk for suicidal thoughts and behaviors in children, adolescents, and young adults 24 years of age and younger receiving an antidepressant versus placebo, with considerable variation in the risk of suicidality among drugs. The difference in absolute risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviors across different indications was highest in those with major depression. The need for an antidepressant in children, adolescents, or young adults for any use must be weighed against the risk of suicidality; it is unknown if this risk extends to long-term use. All patients should be monitored for symptom worsening or suicidality, especially at treatment initiation or after dose changes. Caregivers and/or patients should immediately notify the prescriber of changes in behavior or suicidal ideation. A change to the treatment regimen or discontinuation of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline may be necessary in patients with emerging suicidality or worsening depression.
    Headline
    bipolar disorder
    General Information
    A major depressive episode may be the initial presentation of bipolar disorder, and the use of an antidepressant in people predisposed to bipolar disorder may increase the likelihood of precipitating a mixed or manic episode. Prior to initiating treatment with chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline, people with depressive symptoms should be screened for risk factors for bipolar disorder, including a detailed personal psychiatric history and family history of bipolar, suicide, and/or depression. If a patient taking chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline develops symptoms consistent with mania or hypomania, the medication should be discontinued, and appropriate therapy should be initiated..
    Headline
    coadministration with certain medications, pulmonary disease, respiratory insufficiency
    General Information
    Chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline coadministration with certain medications, such as opioids, may result in serious adverse effects, including profound sedation, CNS depression, respiratory depression, coma, and death. The concomitant use of these medications has demonstrated an increased risk of drug-related mortality compared to the use of opioids alone. The combined use of opioids and benzodiazepines should be reserved for patients in whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If the decision is made to prescribe chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline concomitantly with opioids, the lowest effective dose should be prescribed for the shortest duration of use, and patients should be followed closely for signs and symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Advise patients and caregivers about the risks of respiratory depression and sedation when chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline is used with opioids. Deaths have been reported in people with severe pulmonary disease after initiation of chlordiazepoxide; closely monitor patients with impaired respiratory function (respiratory insufficiency). If signs and symptoms of respiratory depression, hypoventilation, or apnea occur, discontinue chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline.
    Headline
    abrupt discontinuation, increased risk for benzodiazepine withdrawal, substance abuse disorder
    General Information
    The use of benzodiazepines, including chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline, exposes users to serious adverse events, including risks of abuse, misuse, and addiction, which can lead to overdose or death. Abuse and misuse of benzodiazepines commonly involve concomitant use of other medications, alcohol, and/or illicit substances, which can increase the frequency of serious adverse outcomes. Before prescribing and throughout treatment, assess each patient's risk for abuse, misuse, and addiction using a standardized screening tool. Counsel patients about the risks and proper use of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline and prescribe the lowest effective dose to reduce excess exposure and advise patients on proper disposal of unused medication. Minimize the concomitant use of CNS depressants or other substances associated with abuse, misuse, and addiction (e.g., opioid analgesics, stimulants). If a substance abuse disorder is suspected, evaluate the patient and institute or refer them for early treatment. Chronic, continued use of benzodiazepines may also lead to physical benzodiazepine dependence. The risk of dependence increases with longer treatment duration and higher daily doses and may lead to an increased risk for benzodiazepine withdrawal upon discontinuation. Abrupt discontinuation or rapid dose reduction can precipitate acute withdrawal reactions, which can be life-threatening. To reduce the risk of withdrawal reactions, use a gradual taper to reduce the dose or discontinue chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline. In some cases, patients using benzodiazepines have developed a protracted withdrawal syndrome with symptoms lasting weeks to more than 12 months. Patients with a history of a seizure disorder should not be withdrawn abruptly from benzodiazepines due to the risk of precipitating seizures; status epilepticus has also been reported. Clinicians should be aware that the use of flumazenil may increase the risk of seizures, particularly in long-term users of benzodiazepines.
    Headline
    activities requiring coordination and concentration, driving or operating machinery
    General Information
    Chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline may cause significant sedation and has the potential to impair judgment, thinking, or motor skills. Individuals should use caution when driving or operating machinery or participating in other activities requiring coordination and concentration until they know how chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline may affect them. Some patients may experience excessive sedation and impaired ability to perform tasks. Given the primary CNS effects of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline, use caution during coadministration with other CNS depressants. Alcohol may further impair cognitive and motor skills and can increase the risk of serious CNS or respiratory depression and alcohol should be avoided during chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline treatment.
    Headline
    acute myocardial infarction, cardiovascular disease
    General Information
    Chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline is contraindicated in people who are in the acute recovery phase following an acute myocardial infarction due to an increased risk of sudden death. People with cardiovascular disease should be watched closely. Tricyclic antidepressant drugs, particularly when given in high doses, have been reported to produce arrhythmias, sinus tachycardia and prolongation of conduction time. Myocardial infarction and stroke have also been reported in patients receiving drugs of this class.  Use should be avoided in people with Brugada syndrome or suspected of having Brugada syndrome, as tricyclic antidepressants (like amitriptyline) have been reported to cause unmasking of Brugada syndrome, characterized by syncope, abnormal electrocardiographic (ECG) findings, and a risk of sudden death.
    Headline
    prostatic hypertrophy, urinary retention
    General Information
    Because of the atropine-like action of the amitriptyline component, great care should be used in treating patients with a history of urinary retention or with risks for urinary retention, such as people with benign prostatic hypertrophy.
    Headline
    closed-angle glaucoma, narrow iridocorneal angles
    General Information
    Because of the atropine-like action of the amitriptyline component of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline products, use caution in people with a history of closed-angle glaucoma. In patients with angle-closure glaucoma, even average doses may precipitate an attack in patients with anatomically narrow iridocorneal angles who do not have a patent iridectomy.
    Headline
    CYP2D6 poor metabolizer, hepatic disease
    General Information
    The usual precautions in treating people with impaired hepatic function should be observed when chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline is prescribed. Use chlordiazepoxide with caution in people with hepatic disease due to the extensive metabolism of the drug in the liver and the production of active metabolites; the elimination half-life of the drug can be prolonged. For example, chlordiazepoxide metabolites may accumulate, leading to severe and prolonged stupor in patients with hepatic cirrhosis. Patients with hepatic disease should receive reduced initial dosages and be closely monitored for sedation and tolerability. Amitriptyline should be used with caution in people with hepatic impairment or hepatic failure. Caution is also advised when using amitriptyline in people who are a CYP2D6 poor metabolizer, as these individuals may have higher than expected plasma concentrations at usual doses, increasing the risk of adverse effects or toxicity. 72394
    Headline
    renal failure, renal impairment
    General Information
    Use chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline with caution in those with renal impairment. In renal failure (eGFR less than 10 mL/minute), it is suggested to reduce the dosage of chlordiazepoxide by at least 50%. Monitor these patients closely for excessive sedation or other adverse effects. The chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline is only available as a fixed-dose combination, and suggested adjustments may not be feasible.
    Headline
    myasthenia gravis
    General Information
    Chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline should be used with caution in patients with myasthenia gravis, particularly at higher doses; these patients may be more sensitive to the CNS and respiratory effects of the benzodiazepines.
    Headline
    porphyria
    General Information
    Chlordiazepoxide may have a porphyrogenic effect and chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline should be used cautiously in patients with porphyria. There are isolated reports associating chlordiazepoxide with exacerbation of porphyria.
    Headline
    electroconvulsive therapy, seizures
    General Information
    Chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline should be used with caution in people with a preexisting seizure disorder or a history of seizures, as all tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) can lower the seizure threshold. If seizures occur, discontinue amitriptyline. Concurrent administration of electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) and a TCA may increase the hazards associated with such therapy. Concurrent administration of ECT and chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline should be limited to those patients for whom it is essential.
    Headline
    hyperthyroidism
    General Information
    Close supervision is required when chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline is given to patients with hyperthyroidism or in those who are receiving thyroid medication due to increased risks of cardiac arrhythmias.
    Headline
    planned procedure
    General Information
    The chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline prescribing information recommends discontinuation of the medication for several days, or as long as the clinical situation will allow, prior to elective surgery (planned procedure). However, some articles suggest tricyclic antidepressants may be continued in patients with a low risk for arrhythmias. If the medication is not discontinued prior to surgery, close monitoring of the patient is recommended due to the risk of increased sensitivity to catecholamines and an increased risk for cardiovascular effects.
    Headline
    geriatric
    General Information
    Geriatric adults are particularly sensitive to the anticholinergic side effects of tricyclic antidepressants. Because chlordiazepoxide can cause drowsiness and a decreased level of consciousness, there is a higher risk of falls, particularly in the elderly, with the potential for subsequent severe injuries. Dosages of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline should be limited to the smallest effective amount to preclude the development of ataxia, oversedation, confusion or anticholinergic effects. According to the Beers Criteria, TCAs such as amitriptyline are considered potentially inappropriate medications (PIMs) in geriatric adults; in general, avoid use due to the potential for orthostatic hypotension, anticholinergic effects or toxicity, and sedation. Benzodiazepines such as chlordiazepoxide are also considered PIMs. Older adults have an increased sensitivity and slower metabolism of long-acting benzodiazepines such as chlordiazepoxide, which increases their risk of cognitive impairment, delirium, falls, fractures, and motor vehicle accidents. Avoid this combination product in those with the following conditions due to the potential for symptom exacerbation or adverse effects: syncope (increased risk of orthostatic hypotension or bradycardia), dementia/cognitive impairment (drug-induced CNS effects), delirium/high risk of delirium (new-onset or worsening delirium), lower urinary tract symptoms/benign prostatic hyperplasia in men (urinary retention or hesitancy), or history of falls or fractures (ataxia, impaired psychomotor function, syncope, and additional falls). If this product must be used, consider reducing use of other CNS-active medications and implement strategies to reduce fall risk. Because TCAs can cause or exacerbate hyponatremia and SIADH and older adults are at increased risk of developing these conditions, sodium levels should be closely monitored when starting or changing dosages. The U.S. Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act (OBRA) regulates the use of antidepressants in long-term care facilities. TCAs and chlordiazepoxide are rarely medications of choice for mood disorders in older adults. When this combination product is being used to manage behavior, stabilize mood, or treat a psychiatric disorder, tapering as outlined in the OBRA guidelines should be attempted unless clinically contraindicated. Dosages and durations of treatment used should align with prescribing labels, published literature recommendations, and expert guidelines.
    Headline
    pregnancy
    General Information
    Published data from observational studies of benzodiazepine use during pregnancy have not established a drug-associated risk of major birth defects. Early studies suggested an increased risk of congenital malformations with chlordiazepoxide and diazepam use. However, more recent case-control and cohort studies of benzodiazepine use during pregnancy, adjusted for confounding exposures to other substances, have not confirmed these findings. Benzodiazepines cross the placenta. Neonates exposed to benzodiazepines during late pregnancy, including during labor and delivery, may experience symptoms of sedation. Those with prolonged exposure during late pregnancy may experience withdrawal after delivery. The incidence, time to onset, and duration of sedation and withdrawal are multifactorial (e.g., duration of use, drug lipophilicity, placental disposition, degree of accumulation in neonatal tissues). Chlordiazepoxide is not a preferred benzodiazepine to use during pregnancy because of its long duration of action and active metabolites. Individuals who become pregnant or intend to become pregnant while taking chlordiazepoxide should be advised to discuss the possibility of discontinuing the drug with their physician. If a benzodiazepine is required during pregnancy, avoid first trimester administration if possible, consider short-acting agents, limit treatment to the shortest possible duration and lowest effective dose, and discontinue the drug well before delivery. Amitriptyline has been shown to cross the placenta. An epidemiologic study found a slight increase in the risk of preeclampsia when amitriptyline and other tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) were used in the second and third trimesters of pregnancy. While there is no clear association of amitriptyline or other TCAs with birth defects, the use of TCAs throughout pregnancy or near delivery has been associated with a neonatal antidepressant-related withdrawal syndrome.  Although a causal relationship has not been established, there have been a few reports of adverse events, including CNS effects, gastrointestinal defects, limb deformities, or developmental delay, in infants exposed to amitriptyline in utero. Teratogenic effects were not observed in mice, rats, or rabbits when amitriptyline was given orally at doses of 2 to 40 mg/kg/day (up to 13 times the maximum recommended human dose [MRHD]). Studies in the literature have shown amitriptyline to be teratogenic in mice and hamsters when given by various routes of administration at doses of 28 to 100 mg/kg/day (9 to 33 times the MRHD), producing multiple malformations. Another study in the rat reported that an oral dose of 25 mg/kg/day (8 times the MRHD) produced delays in ossification of fetal vertebral bodies without other signs of embryotoxicity. In rabbits, an oral dose of 60 mg/kg/day (20 times the MRHD) was reported to cause incomplete ossification of the cranial bones. There is a pregnancy registry that monitors pregnancy outcomes in people exposed to chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline or other psychiatric medications during pregnancy. Healthcare providers are encouraged to register patients by calling the National Pregnancy Registry for Psychiatric Medications at 1-866-961-2388 or visiting online at https://womensmentalhealth.org/research/pregnancyregistry/.
    Headline
    neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero
    General Information
    Monitor neonates and infants exposed to this medication in utero for symptoms of sedation, poor adaptation, and withdrawal. If symptoms are present, manage accordingly. Benzodiazepine-related effects in neonates exposed during late pregnancy, including during labor and delivery, may include sedation, respiratory depression, lethargy, and hypotonia. Benzodiazepine-related withdrawal symptoms may include hyperreflexia, irritability, restlessness, tremors, inconsolable crying, and feeding difficulties. Antidepressant-related symptoms can arise immediately upon delivery and may include respiratory distress, cyanosis, apnea, seizures, temperature instability, feeding difficulties, vomiting, hypoglycemia, hypotonia, hypertonia, hyperreflexia, tremors, jitteriness, irritability, and constant crying. Complications may require prolonged hospitalization, respiratory support, and tube feeding. These symptoms are consistent with either direct toxic effects of the tricyclic antidepressant or a drug discontinuation syndrome.
    Headline
    breast-feeding
    General Information
    Advise individuals to avoid breast-feeding during the use of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline. Chlordiazepoxide and its metabolites are excreted into human milk in moderate concentrations. Based on its long half-life and active metabolites, other benzodiazepines are preferred in lactation. Monitor children exposed to benzodiazepines through breast milk for excessive sedation, slowed breathing rate, poor feeding, and inadequate weight gain. Withdrawal may occur in those with long-term exposure to the drug. This may occur with cessation of breast-feeding or cessation of the medication in the breast-feeding person. Amitriptyline as a single agent may be used with caution during breast-feeding; monitor the breastfed child for sedation and poor feeding. Amitriptyline is excreted into human milk, but case reports indicate low concentrations of amitriptyline and metabolites (e.g., nortriptyline) in human milk. In one report in which an individual received amitriptyline 100 mg/day while breast-feeding, concentrations of 83 to 141 nanograms/mL were detected in the lactating person's serum. Concentrations of 135 to 151 nanograms/mL were found in the milk, but no trace of the drug could be detected in the child's serum. Other case reports indicated that a breastfed child would receive approximately 0.9% to 1% of the maternal weight-adjusted amitriptyline dosage.

Pregnancy And Lactation
  Pregnancy
    General Information
    Published data from observational studies of benzodiazepine use during pregnancy have not established a drug-associated risk of major birth defects. Early studies suggested an increased risk of congenital malformations with chlordiazepoxide and diazepam use. However, more recent case-control and cohort studies of benzodiazepine use during pregnancy, adjusted for confounding exposures to other substances, have not confirmed these findings. Benzodiazepines cross the placenta. Neonates exposed to benzodiazepines during late pregnancy, including during labor and delivery, may experience symptoms of sedation. Those with prolonged exposure during late pregnancy may experience withdrawal after delivery. The incidence, time to onset, and duration of sedation and withdrawal are multifactorial (e.g., duration of use, drug lipophilicity, placental disposition, degree of accumulation in neonatal tissues). Chlordiazepoxide is not a preferred benzodiazepine to use during pregnancy because of its long duration of action and active metabolites. Individuals who become pregnant or intend to become pregnant while taking chlordiazepoxide should be advised to discuss the possibility of discontinuing the drug with their physician. If a benzodiazepine is required during pregnancy, avoid first trimester administration if possible, consider short-acting agents, limit treatment to the shortest possible duration and lowest effective dose, and discontinue the drug well before delivery. Amitriptyline has been shown to cross the placenta. An epidemiologic study found a slight increase in the risk of preeclampsia when amitriptyline and other tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) were used in the second and third trimesters of pregnancy. While there is no clear association of amitriptyline or other TCAs with birth defects, the use of TCAs throughout pregnancy or near delivery has been associated with a neonatal antidepressant-related withdrawal syndrome.  Although a causal relationship has not been established, there have been a few reports of adverse events, including CNS effects, gastrointestinal defects, limb deformities, or developmental delay, in infants exposed to amitriptyline in utero. Teratogenic effects were not observed in mice, rats, or rabbits when amitriptyline was given orally at doses of 2 to 40 mg/kg/day (up to 13 times the maximum recommended human dose [MRHD]). Studies in the literature have shown amitriptyline to be teratogenic in mice and hamsters when given by various routes of administration at doses of 28 to 100 mg/kg/day (9 to 33 times the MRHD), producing multiple malformations. Another study in the rat reported that an oral dose of 25 mg/kg/day (8 times the MRHD) produced delays in ossification of fetal vertebral bodies without other signs of embryotoxicity. In rabbits, an oral dose of 60 mg/kg/day (20 times the MRHD) was reported to cause incomplete ossification of the cranial bones. There is a pregnancy registry that monitors pregnancy outcomes in people exposed to chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline or other psychiatric medications during pregnancy. Healthcare providers are encouraged to register patients by calling the National Pregnancy Registry for Psychiatric Medications at 1-866-961-2388 or visiting online at https://womensmentalhealth.org/research/pregnancyregistry/.
  Breastfeeding
    General Information
    Advise individuals to avoid breast-feeding during the use of chlordiazepoxide; amitriptyline. Chlordiazepoxide and its metabolites are excreted into human milk in moderate concentrations. Based on its long half-life and active metabolites, other benzodiazepines are preferred in lactation. Monitor children exposed to benzodiazepines through breast milk for excessive sedation, slowed breathing rate, poor feeding, and inadequate weight gain. Withdrawal may occur in those with long-term exposure to the drug. This may occur with cessation of breast-feeding or cessation of the medication in the breast-feeding person. Amitriptyline as a single agent may be used with caution during breast-feeding; monitor the breastfed child for sedation and poor feeding. Amitriptyline is excreted into human milk, but case reports indicate low concentrations of amitriptyline and metabolites (e.g., nortriptyline) in human milk. In one report in which an individual received amitriptyline 100 mg/day while breast-feeding, concentrations of 83 to 141 nanograms/mL were detected in the lactating person's serum. Concentrations of 135 to 151 nanograms/mL were found in the milk, but no trace of the drug could be detected in the child's serum. Other case reports indicated that a breastfed child would receive approximately 0.9% to 1% of the maternal weight-adjusted amitriptyline dosage.

Interactions
Abiraterone: (Moderate) Monitor for an increase in amitriptyline-related adverse reactions if coadministration with abiraterone is necessary; a dose reduction of amitriptyline may be necessary. Amitriptyline is a CYP2D6 substrate and abiraterone is a moderate CYP2D6 inhibitor. Patients who are stable on a given dose of amitriptyline may become abruptly toxic when given abiraterone is concomitant therapy.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin, ASA; Caffeine: (Minor) Patients taking benzodiazepines for insomnia should not use caffeine-containing products prior to going to bed as these products may antagonize the sedative effects of the benzodiazepine.
Acetaminophen; Aspirin; diphenhydrAMINE: (Moderate) Coadministration can potentiate the CNS effects (e.g., increased sedation or respiratory depression) of either agent. Use caution with this combination. (Moderate) Monitor for unusual drowsiness and sedation, urinary retention, and reduced gastric motility during coadministration of diphenhydramine and tricyclic antidepressants. Concomitant use may result in additive CNS depression or anticholinergic effects.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine: (Minor) Patients taking benzodiazepines for insomnia should not use caffeine-containing products prior to going to bed as these products may antagonize the sedative effects of the benzodiazepine.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Dihydrocodeine: (Major) Concomitant use of opiate agonists with benzodiazepines may cause respiratory depression, hypotension, profound sedation, and death. Limit the use of opiate pain medications with benzodiazepines to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations needed to achieve the desired clinical effect. If an opiate agonist is initiated in a patient taking a benzodiazepine, use a lower initial dose of the opiate and titrate to clinical response. If a benzodiazepine is prescribed for an indication other than epilepsy in a patient taking an opiate agonist, use a lower initial dose of the benzodiazepine and titrate to clinical response. Educate patients about the risks and symptoms of respiratory depression and sedation. Avoid prescribing opiate cough medications in patients taking benzodiazepines. (Major) Concomitant use of opioid agonists with tricyclic antidepressants may cause excessive sedation and somnolence. Limit the use of opioid pain medications with tricyclic antidepressants to only patients for whom alternative treatment options are inadequate. If concurrent use is necessary, reduce initial dosage and titrate to clinical response; use the lowest effective doses and minimum treatment durations. Also monitor patients for the emergence of serotonin syndrome. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs. The concomitant use of opioids with other drugs that affect the serotonergic neurotransmitter system has resulted in serotonin syndrome. Avoid prescribing opioid cough medication in patients taking tricyclic antidepressants. (Minor) Patients taking benzodiazepines for insomnia should not use caffeine-containing products prior to going to bed as these products may antagonize the sedative effects of the benzodiazepine.
Acetaminophen; Caffeine; Pyrilamine: (Moderate) Additive anticholinergic and CNS effects may be seen when tricyclic antidepressants are used concomitantly with sedating H1-blockers. Antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. (Moderate) Coadministration can potentiate the CNS effects (e.g., increased sedation or respiratory depression) of either agent. Use caution with this combination. (Minor) Patients taking benzodiazepines for insomnia should not use caffeine-containing products prior to going to bed as these products may antagonize the sedative effects of the benzodiazepine.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine: (Moderate) Additive anticholinergic and CNS effects may be seen when tricyclic antidepressants are used concomitantly with sedating H1-blockers. Antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. (Moderate) Coadministration can potentiate the CNS effects (e.g., increased sedation or respiratory depression) of either agent. Use caution with this combination.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Dextromethorphan: (Moderate) Additive anticholinergic and CNS effects may be seen when tricyclic antidepressants are used concomitantly with sedating H1-blockers. Antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. (Moderate) Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering dextromethorphan with tricyclic antidepressants. Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustments. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs. (Moderate) Coadministration can potentiate the CNS effects (e.g., increased sedation or respiratory depression) of either agent. Use caution with this combination.
Acetaminophen; Chlorpheniramine; Dextromethorphan; Phenylephrine: (Major) Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) may markedly enhance the pressor response to parenteral direct-acting sympathomimetic agents such as norepinephrine and, to a lesser extent, epinephrine and phenylephrine. TCAs inhibit norepinephrine reuptake in adrenergic neurons, resulting in increased stimulation of adrenergic receptors. Clinically, the patient might experience hypertension, headache, tremor, palpitations, chest pain, or irregular heartbeat. (Moderate) Additive anticholinergic and CNS effects may be seen when tricyclic antidepressants are used concomitantly with sedating H1-blockers. Antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. (Moderate) Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering dextromethorphan with tricyclic antidepressants. Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustments. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs. (Moderate) Coadministration can potentiate the CNS effects (e.g., increased sedation or respiratory depression) of either agent. Use caution with this combination. (Moderate) The therapeutic effect of phenylephrine may be decreased in patients receiving benzodiazepines. Monitor patients for decreased pressor effect if these agents are administered concomitantly.
